**Purpose:**

This Command Center is a process archive for my AI + Design final project. It contains my research, notes, AI prompt experiments, model outputs, screenshots, development progress, decisions, pivots, and final project materials.

## Project Overview

### **Developing Games with AI as Player Two:**\
Can AI make game development more accessible for beginners without taking away their creative voice?

**Short Summary:**

This project explores how AI can help beginners start developing their own playable games without any prior advanced coding skills. I am investigating whether AI can act as a supportive tool without diminishing the designer's own voice, choices, and imagination. This project will focus on accessibility, creative ownership, and the tension between assistance and authorship.

**Audience and Context:**

This project is for beginner game creators, design students, and artists/storytellers who have game ideas but may feel blocked by coding and technical barriers. This is being developed in the context of an AI + Design class, where I am researching how AI changes the creative process in game development.

**Possible final directions**

- **Playable prototype:** a small game created with AI support by someone with limited game development experience.

- **Process journal:** a documented record of the creative process where AI helped, where it failed, and where I rejected or revised its ideas.

- **Beginner toolkit:** a guide or workflow showing how non-coders can use AI while keeping creative control.

## Inquiry Brief

**Research Question:**

How can AI help beginners develop playable games while still preserving their creative voice?

**Why is Matters:**

Game development can feel unapproachable to people who have strong ideas for mechanics, storytelling, and/or world-building but lack coding or technical skills. AI tools may close that gap by assisting with coding, art, writing, prototyping, and problem-solving. However, this raises another issue. If AI contributes too much to the process, does the final game still feel like it belongs to the creator?

**What I Need to Learn:**

I need to learn how beginner-friendly AI game development tools actually are, especially for someone without much coding experience. I also need to understand where AI is most useful in the process of game-making, whether it's brainstorming, coding, etc... Most importantly, I need to learn how to recognize when AI supports creative direction vs. when it starts replacing it.

**Possible Final Outcomes:**

1. The AI works well enough to the point where it can help a beginner develop a playable game prototype with minimal complications.

2. The AI either does not help with the game-making process, complicates it further, or strays off and completely takes over the creator's vision.

## Defined Deliverables

**What I set out to produce**

I set out to create a small playable game prototype using AI as a support tool, while documenting whether AI helped me learn game development without taking over my creative voice.

**Planned formats**

- Playable GDevelop prototype

- Process journal / weekly progress archive

- Prompt log showing AI interactions and outputs

- Research/source notes

- Screenshots and videos of development progress

- Final project website materials

**Criteria guiding my outputs**

- The game must be playable enough to show a basic loop.

- AI can help with technical support, debugging, prompting, and organization.

- The core game concept, design choices, and creative direction must remain mine.

- Process evidence must show both successes and problems.

- Final materials should explain what AI helped with, what I changed, and what I rejected.

**Current final direction**

A GDevelop dungeon-crawler / bullet-hell prototype supported by a documented AI-assisted development process.

# Game Plan

## Top Priority

## Priority 1: Player Combat Prototype

Start here because everything else depends on whether moving, aiming, and shooting feels good.

Build:

**WASD movement**\
**mouse aiming**\
**left-click shooting**\
**basic projectile object**\
**enemy takes damage from projectile**

Do not worry about dungeon generation yet. Just use one test room. If this part feels bad, the whole game feels bad.

## Priority 2: Basic Enemy System

Add your two first enemy types:

**Chaser enemy:** slime/bat that moves toward the player and deals contact damage.\
**Ranged enemy:** skeleton archer that shoots projectiles at intervals.

Also add:

**player damage**\
**temporary invincibility after hit**\
**enemy health**\
**enemy death**

This proves the combat challenge.

**Priority 2.5**

Temporary Character/Enemy Models:

The reason: you need **some visual identity early enough** to understand hitboxes, readability, scale, and combat feel. But you do **not** need final art before the systems work.

I’d treat this as two phases:

**Temporary models first:** simple pixel sprites for the player, slime/bat, and skeleton archer. These should be readable, not perfect.

**Final models later:** better animations, polished silhouettes, effects, and dungeon-style details.

## Priority 3: Room Clear Logic

Once enemies work, build the room rule:

**room starts active/locked → enemies must be defeated → room becomes cleared → doors unlock**

This is the backbone of your dungeon loop. Even before random generation, this system needs to work in a controlled test floor.

## Priority 4: Small Manual Floor

Before random layout, make a simple connected floor with about **4 rooms**.

Build:

**one large connected map**\
**doors on each side**\
**blocked doors where no room exists**\
**open paths where rooms connect**\
**one item room**\
**one way-down room**

This lets you test the feeling of exploring a floor without fighting the random generation monster too early. That monster wears a tiny wizard hat and eats deadlines.

## Priority 5: Floor Transition

Add the way down:

**player touches stairs/ladder/hole → floor number increases → floor resets or loads new layout**

At first, the “new floor” can be the same layout reused. The important part is proving the loop:

**floor 1 → descend → floor 2 → enemies scale → continue**

## Priority 6: Upgrade System

Start with stat upgrades only:

**increase damage**\
**increase max health or heal**\
**increase fire rate later**

Then add the first weapon-style upgrade:

**shotgun spread projectile**

This should come after the basic projectile system is stable.

## Priority 7: Simple Randomized Floor Layout

Only after the manual 4-room floor works, make the layout random.

Your safest version is:

**choose 4 room positions from a small grid**\
**make sure all rooms connect**\
**choose one item room**\
**choose one way-down room**\
**spawn pre-made room templates**

The creative room designs stay yours. The AI can help with the technical event logic for placing and connecting them.

## Priority 8: Polish and Later Features

Back burner features:

**shops**\
**currency**\
**permanent upgrades**\
**biomes**\
**more enemy variants**\
**more attack patterns**\
**extra end-run stats**\
**environmental storytelling**

These are good ideas, but not needed for the first playable version.

## Lowest Priority

## AI Assisted Creative Process

Prompt: After using GDevelop for a little bit, I think it's time to use that experience and start developing my final deliverable; my first, fully fledged game. I'm working on the planning portion of my game. I need to organize what I'll be doing for my game. I will need you to give me questions, one at a time, asking me what my game is about. Basically this is the creative process and the questions could be the game loop, the mechanics, the story — anything that'll help me organize the parts of my game. After enough questions have been asked and answered, help me organize them into what you think I should prioritize making first. I want to keep this creatively mine, so if you have suggestions, make sure they mainly focus on the technical parts while avoiding any creative vision.

### Your Game Plan So Far

Your game is a **top-down roguelike dungeon crawler** where the player tries to descend as far as possible through connected dungeon floors.

The core loop is:

**explore floor → enter rooms → fight enemies → clear rooms → unlock paths → find item room or way down → descend → repeat until death**

Your main gameplay identity is:

**WASD movement + mouse aiming + projectile combat + enemy bullet dodging**

The player starts with **10 health**, takes more damage from contact enemies than projectiles, and can rarely heal through a special room. This makes damage feel punishing, which supports your goal of careful movement and avoiding sloppy mistakes.

- 4 connected rooms on one scene

- One room that can later become an Upgrade room

- One room that can later become the way-down room

# Game Plan

## Top Priority

## Priority 1: Player Combat Prototype

Start here because everything else depends on whether moving, aiming, and shooting feels good.

Build:

**WASD movement**\
**mouse aiming**\
**left-click shooting**\
**basic projectile object**\
**enemy takes damage from projectile**

Do not worry about dungeon generation yet. Just use one test room. If this part feels bad, the whole game feels bad.

## Priority 2: Basic Enemy System

Add your two first enemy types:

**Chaser enemy:** slime/bat that moves toward the player and deals contact damage.\
**Ranged enemy:** skeleton archer that shoots projectiles at intervals.

Also add:

**player damage**\
**temporary invincibility after hit**\
**enemy health**\
**enemy death**

This proves the combat challenge.

**Priority 2.5**

Temporary Character/Enemy Models:

The reason: you need **some visual identity early enough** to understand hitboxes, readability, scale, and combat feel. But you do **not** need final art before the systems work.

I’d treat this as two phases:

**Temporary models first:** simple pixel sprites for the player, slime/bat, and skeleton archer. These should be readable, not perfect.

**Final models later:** better animations, polished silhouettes, effects, and dungeon-style details.

## Priority 3: Room Clear Logic

Once enemies work, build the room rule:

**room starts active/locked → enemies must be defeated → room becomes cleared → doors unlock**

This is the backbone of your dungeon loop. Even before random generation, this system needs to work in a controlled test floor.

## Priority 4: Small Manual Floor

Before random layout, make a simple connected floor with about **4 rooms**.

Build:

**one large connected map**\
**doors on each side**\
**blocked doors where no room exists**\
**open paths where rooms connect**\
**one item room**\
**one way-down room**

This lets you test the feeling of exploring a floor without fighting the random generation monster too early. That monster wears a tiny wizard hat and eats deadlines.

## Priority 5: Floor Transition

Add the way down:

**player touches stairs/ladder/hole → floor number increases → floor resets or loads new layout**

At first, the “new floor” can be the same layout reused. The important part is proving the loop:

**floor 1 → descend → floor 2 → enemies scale → continue**

## Priority 6: Upgrade System

Start with stat upgrades only:

**increase damage**\
**increase max health or heal**\
**increase fire rate later**

Then add the first weapon-style upgrade:

**shotgun spread projectile**

This should come after the basic projectile system is stable.

## Priority 7: Simple Randomized Floor Layout

Only after the manual 4-room floor works, make the layout random.

Your safest version is:

**choose 4 room positions from a small grid**\
**make sure all rooms connect**\
**choose one item room**\
**choose one way-down room**\
**spawn pre-made room templates**

The creative room designs stay yours. The AI can help with the technical event logic for placing and connecting them.

## Priority 8: Polish and Later Features

Back burner features:

**shops**\
**currency**\
**permanent upgrades**\
**biomes**\
**more enemy variants**\
**more attack patterns**\
**extra end-run stats**\
**environmental storytelling**

These are good ideas, but not needed for the first playable version.

## Lowest Priority

## AI Assisted Creative Process

Prompt: After using GDevelop for a little bit, I think it's time to use that experience and start developing my final deliverable; my first, fully fledged game. I'm working on the planning portion of my game. I need to organize what I'll be doing for my game. I will need you to give me questions, one at a time, asking me what my game is about. Basically this is the creative process and the questions could be the game loop, the mechanics, the story — anything that'll help me organize the parts of my game. After enough questions have been asked and answered, help me organize them into what you think I should prioritize making first. I want to keep this creatively mine, so if you have suggestions, make sure they mainly focus on the technical parts while avoiding any creative vision.

### Your Game Plan So Far

Your game is a **top-down roguelike dungeon crawler** where the player tries to descend as far as possible through connected dungeon floors.

The core loop is:

**explore floor → enter rooms → fight enemies → clear rooms → unlock paths → find item room or way down → descend → repeat until death**

Your main gameplay identity is:

**WASD movement + mouse aiming + projectile combat + enemy bullet dodging**

The player starts with **10 health**, takes more damage from contact enemies than projectiles, and can rarely heal through a special room. This makes damage feel punishing, which supports your goal of careful movement and avoiding sloppy mistakes.

## Final Deliverables / Website Materials

**Playable Prototype**  

A GDevelop dungeon-crawler / bullet-hell prototype showing the basic game loop: move, aim, shoot, fight enemies, clear rooms, and continue building toward floor progression.

**Process Archive**  

This Milanote board documents research, weekly progress, screenshots, prompt experiments, AI outputs, decisions, pivots, and reflections.

**Final Project Site / Dashboard**  

A website or dashboard that presents the project clearly for final review, including the prototype, research summary, AI process, and reflection.

**Final Reflection**  

A written reflection explaining where AI helped, where it failed, what I accepted or rejected, and how I preserved creative control.

[20260524-0435-23.4233100.mp4](https://media.milanote.com/p/files/1Wr0zg1w6Bq874/sSu/20260524-0435-23.4233100.mp4) (12.6 MB)

Evidence item:

Manual floor structure and room logic debugging

Artifact:

Screenshots and video directly below — 4-room test floor and room boundary debugging

What it shows:

The prototype uses a connected room layout where the player can move between rooms on one scene. It also shows the debugging process for making each room manage its own boundaries and triggers.

Why it matters:

This proves the dungeon structure before adding random generation, keeping the project manageable while still moving toward the final roguelike loop. This also became an important AI limitation moment, where the AI Agent kept treating the Room boundaries too generally, while I was able to identify that each room needed its own Room ID and specific variables. This shows that I wasn't just following AI instructions, but that I was also learning enough to recognize and point the AI towards the issue myself.

## Original AI-Suggested Roadmap

## AI-Suggested Week 5

**Goal**  

Define the project focus and organize the research foundation.

**Activities**  

\- Finalize project identity, inquiry brief, and source starter list.

\- Read and annotate the GDC generative AI source.

\- Identify the main tension: AI as creative support vs. AI as creative replacement.

\- Create folders/sections in Milanote for research, prompts, experiments, screenshots, and reflections.

**Tools**  

Milanote, ChatGPT, GDC PDF, class notes

**Output**  

A cleaned-up Command Center with project identity, inquiry brief, and starter sources.

**Success indicator**  

Someone can open the board and understand what I am investigating, why it matters, and where my research starts.

## AI-Suggested Week 6

**Goal**  

Research how AI is currently being used in game development.

**Activities**  

\- Gather notes from 2–3 reliable sources.

\- Pull out examples of AI being used for brainstorming, coding, prototyping, assets, and debugging.

\- Separate examples into two categories: “AI supports creativity” and “AI risks replacing creativity.”

\- Begin a simple glossary of key terms: generative AI, prototyping, creative voice, authorship, no-code tools.

**Tools**  

Milanote, GDC report, GDevelop, ChatGPT, possibly YouTube or article sources

**Output**  

A research notes section with quotes, summaries, screenshots, and source links.

**Success indicator**  

I can explain at least three ways AI helps game development and at least three concerns developers have about it.

## AI-Suggested Week 7

**Goal**  

Test beginner-friendly AI game development tools.

**Activities**  

\- Choose 1–2 tools to test, such as GDevelop, ChatGPT, or another beginner-friendly game tool.

\- Try basic AI-assisted tasks: generate a game idea, create a mechanic, write simple code/logic, or troubleshoot a problem.

\- Screenshot each experiment.

\- Record what AI helped with and where I still had to make decisions.

**Tools**  

GDevelop, ChatGPT, Milanote, screen capture

**Output**  

A first set of AI workflow experiments with prompts, outputs, screenshots, and short reflections.

**Success indicator**  

I have evidence showing what AI can realistically help a beginner do.

## AI-Suggested Week 8

**Goal**  

Choose the game concept and define the prototype scope.

**Activities**  

\- Review experiment results and decide what kind of small game is realistic.

\- Define the game’s core loop: what the player does repeatedly.

\- Create a simple game design outline: premise, player goal, mechanic, visual style, and win/lose condition.

\- Decide what parts AI is allowed to help with and what parts must stay personally directed.

**Tools**  

Milanote, ChatGPT, sketches, GDevelop or chosen game tool

**Output**  

A one-page mini game design document and a clear prototype plan.

**Success indicator**  

The game idea is small enough to build, but still connected to my research question about AI and creative voice.

## AI-Suggested Week 9

**Goal**  

Build the first playable prototype.

**Activities**  

\- Start building the basic version of the game.

\- Use AI for help with coding, logic, debugging, placeholder writing, or planning.

\- Document every major AI interaction: prompt, response, decision, result.

\- Mark moments where I accepted, changed, or rejected AI suggestions.

**Tools**  

GDevelop or chosen engine, ChatGPT, Milanote, screenshots/video capture

**Output**  

A rough playable prototype and an AI process log.

**Success indicator**  

The prototype can be played, even if it is unfinished or visually simple.

## AI-Suggested Week 10

**Goal**  

Playtest, revise, and evaluate creative ownership.

**Activities**  

\- Have classmates or friends test the prototype.

\- Ask feedback questions about clarity, originality, and whether the game feels personally designed.

\- Revise the prototype based on feedback.

\- Reflect on where AI helped most and where it may have pulled the project away from my original intent.

**Tools**  

Prototype, feedback form or notes, Milanote, ChatGPT

**Output**  

A revised prototype, feedback notes, and a reflection on AI’s role in the creative process.

**Success indicator**  

I can point to specific examples where my creative decisions shaped the game more than the AI did.

## AI-Suggested Week 11

**Goal**  

Prepare the final presentation and process documentation.

**Activities**  

\- Finalize the prototype or documented experiment.

\- Organize screenshots, prompts, research notes, and reflections.

\- Create a final explanation of what I learned about AI, beginner game development, and creative voice.

\- Decide whether the final outcome is best framed as a prototype, process journal, toolkit, or combination.

**Tools**  

Milanote, prototype tool, ChatGPT, screenshots/video, presentation software if needed

**Output**  

Final Command Center, playable prototype or documented prototype, and final reflection.

**Success indicator**  

Someone can understand what I researched, what I tested, what I made, how AI was used, and what I learned about keeping creative control.
